ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-15.ana.ud2571e294	In the spring of 2011, the Middle East was marked by hope. A generation of young people stood up in the demand for democracy and freedom. Dictatorships fell, and democratic forces prevailed. We talked about an Arab spring. But it never became an Arabic summer. Today, three years later, the Arab Spring has become an ice cold winter, and it has been a long time since the situation in the Middle East has been more difficult. In Egypt, the generals are back in the Presidential Palace. The people of Bahrain have not gained their freedom. This summer the people of Gaza were once again under bombing, and a negotiated solution between Israel and Palestine is further away than for a long time. In Syria, we are witnessing the greatest humanitarian disaster in many decades. The death tolls exceed our worst nightmare, and millions of people are driven away. With the IS mobilization in Syria and Iraq, we see abuses that are unattainable. This is a serious picture, and Norway needs to engage. This concerns us. It concerns our own security, but above all – it concerns our common human solidarity. And that's in this image of chaos and disasters it's also important to take care of what actually works. We must have our attention directed also to where it does not burn the most. Lebanon has a history of civil war and conflicts with neighbouring countries, but is today one of the countries that have actually had stability. It is perhaps the most modern and democratic Arab country. It is a country that needs our attention, because almost no matter what we discuss about the Middle East, Lebanon has a role to play. NRK correspondent Odd Karsten Tveit has called Lebanon the Middle East theatre scene. Everything that happens in the Middle East has consequences for Lebanon, everything that happens in Lebanon, has consequences for the rest of the region. That is why I wanted to have this debate – to hear the foreign minister's view of our work in Lebanon and what we can do to be involved and ensure stability and security in this country. Norway has a love band for Lebanon. Thousands of young boys and girls have served in UNIFIL forces. When we pulled out in 1998, there were over 20,000 Northmen who had been in Lebanon with the blue helmets to protect peace. It is little, if anything, that has changed Norwegian Middle East policy more than the stories of what these have experienced. When they left, Norway was one of the most Israel-friendly countries in Europe, but their stories of Israel’s brutal abuse gradually changed its position and became one of the countries that spoke the Palestinians most often. Our forces in Lebanon have been important for Norwegian foreign policy. That's one part of our connection. The other is all those who have been in Lebanon and who have worked with solidarity, who have run hospitals through NORWAC, who have cleared mines through Norwegian National Aid, or who have run refugee camps with the Refugee Aid. Many Nordics have been very committed and have contributed to important humanitarian work. Norway has been there for our friends in Lebanon, now they need us more than for a long time. There are few countries in the world that are more under pressure than they are, with conflicts on all sides and with internal mobilization in their own country. The war in Syria means that both Hezbollah, who supports Assad, and the foreign fighters of the IS constitute a serious threat to Lebanon’s stability. In the summer, there were three suicides in Beirut's center in one week, one of them in the middle of the center. Fortunately, police forces were able to minimize civilian losses, but there was a serious warning of the dangers these groups pose. IS must be combated at all levels. The Workers' Party has therefore supported military participation in the fight against this terrorist group. The concern is that it is the debate on the military to get all our attention. To win this fight, we have to meet the IS in all areas. They must meet politically, humanitarianly and civilly. This is at least as important as the military, and it is also important to be present in those areas that they haven't really got rooty so far, but where it can really go wrong. That is why Lebanon must take a holistic view of the fight against extremism. What role does the Lebanon Minister of Foreign Affairs think can play in the fight against terrorism and extremism, and how can we protect them from going outside the trap? Today there is political paralysis in Lebanon. Following the assassination of Rafiq Hariri in 2005, the political system has been fragmented and fragmented. There have been two main fractions, mainly based on the influence of Syria. For periods, the country has not had a government, and elections have been postponed. Now, as those most in need of a uniting and powerful set of rules, they stand weak and split back. It's a challenge that can lead to collapse. The greatest impact on Lebanon of the war in Syria is the huge refugee flows. Lebanon has not ratified the United Nations Convention on Refugees. Nevertheless, few countries in the world have done more for refugees than just them. Over the last three years, more than one million Syrian refugees have settled in the country. They now account for a quarter of the country's population. Clearly, this puts our own debates on refugee policy in a quite different perspective. To show the relationship of strength, it's like all the inhabitants of Canada and Mexico would live in the United States in three years. It is hardly possible to explain and understand the challenges this poses — social problems, unemployment, social dumping, crime and political unrest. The world must stand up for Syria's refugees. We must support Lebanon and other neighbouring countries in their important contribution. We need to support UN organisations such as the United Nations. High Commissioner for Refugees and UNRWA. The refugees have come out of the war, but what future do they have? There is nothing to suggest that they can return home in a very, very long time. How do we ensure that their rights are respected? How do we give children hope for a future? Do we put up with education and health, or do we make them eternal refugees? We can talk about generations that can be lost if the world does not respond. We are going to stand up to them in neighbouring countries – for Lebanon's more than one million refugees, for those in Jordan, Turkey and Iraq. But we also have to do more ourselves. Europe must stand up, both because we can, and simply because it is full in neighbouring countries. There's nowhere to be anymore. I am aware that it is not the Minister for Foreign Affairs who is responsible for how many Syrian refugees Norway should accept, but I hope he is committed to the matter, both here at home and in meetings with his colleagues in other European countries. The whole of Europe must stand up. It's important how many we accept, but maybe the most important thing is who is what we accept? How can we be a relief? We believe that we must accept those who need it the most, those who have the worst in the camps in Lebanon – injured, disabled, those with the greatest traumas. That is why I hope the Minister for Foreign Affairs is committed internally to the government on how Norway can do more. Later today, the Stortinget will discuss why the government rejected the United Nations' request to accept over 100 injured Syrian refugees. We have heard that the argument is the capacity of Norwegian municipalities. I hope the people of Lebanon did not hear that answer. The Syrian refugees in Lebanon come to the top of the 200,000-300,000 Palestinian refugees who have been there for decades. Unfortunately, history will often have it that it is the Palestinian refugees who come to the very bottom of the ranking when conflicts occur in the Middle East. That's the way it is now. In Lebanon, the Palestinian refugees have ended up as eternal refugees with very limited rights to work and residence. They live in crowded refugee camps without hope for the future. Most of them live in poverty, part of them in extreme poverty. There's no reason to think they'll get more rights in the years to come. On the contrary, the situation of the Syrian refugees has made it worse for them. These are forgotten by the world – yes, if they are not forgotten, they are at least ignored. We can't allow there to be hundreds of thousands of people who live for decades without completely elementary rights. How does the Minister of Foreign Affairs engage with the Palestinian refugees in Lebanon? Lebanon is a hard-pressed country with both internal unrest and conflicts around it on all sides. They should be part of the solution to the political and humanitarian crises in the area, not a strengthening of the problem. Then they need more space on the political map – here in Europe, here in Norway. This debate is thus a small contribution to doing just that, and I look forward to hearing the Foreign Minister's assessments.
ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-15.ana.ud2571e330	Lebanon is hit hard by the war in Syria. A number of actors are fighting each other or coordinating operations in the border area between Lebanon and Syria. I therefore fully share the concern of the representative, Mr Aukarust, and thank you for an important initiative through this interpellation. The conflict in Syria and its flow of refugees is a great burden for Lebanon. Lebanon takes the brunt of the burden of refugees from Syria, both in absolute terms and in relation to its own population and area. Nearly one third of the population of Lebanon are now Syrian refugees. According to the UN High Commissioner for Refugees, Lebanon is the world's most refugee country in relation to its own population. So to say all the refugees live outside the camps. Their presence naturally creates challenges, not least because the situation leads to increasing scarcity of resources. A majority of the refugees are in the poorest areas of the country. Infrastructure such as water supply, roads and electricity is poorly developed and not available. I myself have been in Lebanon and seen this, and Norway has stepped up our aid and our humanitarian support considerably over the past year and a half. Refugee flows put pressure on already limited resources and lead to greater labour market congestion. Some of the same features are also seen in Jordan. In this situation, of course, it is essential that Norway and the international community help Lebanon to meet the needs of refugees. At the same time, we must help Lebanon to fulfil its obligations to its own citizens. We must do this for humanitarian reasons. We must also support political stability and counteract radicalisation, as the Aukust representative was also concerned with. The Government prioritises humanitarian aid for victims of the Syrian and Iraqi conflicts and support for communities receiving large displaced groups. Together with regional and international actors, including the UN, we are working for a political solution in Syria and the region. This work is demanding. But it is also absolutely necessary. Only a political solution can provide the basis for positive developments in Lebanon and the region. Norwegian participation in the International Coalition to Combat ISIL in Iraq is part of this picture. The military capacity building to which we are going to contribute in Iraq intends to make the country more resilient to extremism. This is part of our efforts to ensure security and stability in Lebanon and other neighbouring countries. Norway shall continue to be among the largest humanitarian contributors to Syria. The total Syria response for 2014 amounts to nearly 1 billion. kr, broken down by humanitarian aid for Syrian refugees, for removing chemical weapons and not least for so-called neighbouring country aid. And there's about 220 million. humanitarian aid to Iraq. The action in Lebanon is channelled, inter alia: through Norwegian non-governmental organisations such as the Refugee Aid, the Red Barna, the Norwegian People's Aid and the Norwegian Aid Committee, as well as the UN and the Red Cross. We pay particular attention to the distribution of emergency aid, shelter, water and sanitation, education and health care. The government has a specific focus on education, including support for the UNICEF No Lost Generation strategy. The majority of Norwegian support to neighbouring countries is humanitarian. However, funding is also provided for longer-term actions, both through the World Bank, the United Nations and through direct long-term support. Last week Norway gave 25 million. kr to the World Bank's Syria response. Twenty million. This will primarily be used in Lebanon. This is going to add up to 10 million. kr to the World Bank’s Multi-stakeholder Fund for Lebanon and Jordan, which was granted earlier this year. The aim of our support for Lebanon is to assist Syrian refugees in the country and to contribute to increased political stability in the light of the Syrian conflict. It is then important to enable Lebanon to provide basic services to its own population. Norway was the first country to respond to the call to contribute to a stabilisation fund for Lebanon by taking the lead in the creation of the fund at all through the World Bank. There was an important meeting in Paris this spring, and Norway will continue to press for this MIF to be the basis for building a Lebanon capable of coping with the ongoing crisis. As the only country, we contributed directly to the work of the Lebanese authorities with the Syrian refugees, through cooperation with the Lebanese Social Ministry. Many Northmen have a close relationship with Lebanon, on which the interpellant was involved in its inception. More than 21,000 Northmen participated from the establishment of the UN peacekeeping force in Lebanon, UNIFIL, until the Norwegian personnel contribution was finally completed in 2009. We continue to contribute to the funding of UNIFIL. Only in the last six years does this amount to nearly 150 million. kr – no later than 29 mill. kr in year. Norway has been running an oil for development programme in Lebanon over a number of years. Norway has stressed the need to engage in an open and broad political dialogue with all the political actors in Lebanon, which we believe is a positive contribution to the stability of the country. Against this background, including arenas for political discussion in Lebanon have also been established. In 2014, about 300 people from Lebanon were resettled in Norway. In the budgetary agreement with the Christian People's Party and the Left, we agreed to increase the quota for the next year from 1,000 to 1,500 Syrian refugees. Many of these are expected to be taken from Lebanon. Despite our efforts and the efforts of many other countries, there is unfortunately no prospect of an immediate improvement in the situation in the region. The government will continue its efforts to meet the needs of the refugees and their host communities. We will do what we can to build under the political stability of Lebanon and the rest of the region. Together with other countries, we will contribute to the fight against ISIL by military, political, ideological and economic means.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-15.ana.ud2571e397	I would also like to thank the inter-pellant August for taking up the situation in Lebanon, which is the neighbouring country that is most aware of the consequences of the cruel Syrian civil war. The situation of Syrian refugees in the region is precarious. More than three million have fled since the start of the war, and more than one third, i.e. more than 1.1 million, have fled to neighboring Lebanon. The consequences of this for Lebanon are dramatic: the infrastructure capacity is far beyond the gap point, many refugees are left to shed and abandoned construction sites, the water and electricity supply for the population is inadequate, the health care system in the country is unable to accept all those with acute and serious illness, and in addition there is an increase in violence, crime and the struggle for jobs where cheap Syrian labour competes. For any country it will be extremely demanding to absorb over one million refugees. This is particularly difficult for Lebanon because of the underlying and lasting problems in the country. Although Lebanon has a constitution, problems have been encountered in establishing a stable political system and governance. It's partly due to Hizbollah's strong influence through military militias. Although Syria formally withdrew from Lebanon in 2005, it has continued to undermine its role by interfering in elections and preventing the development of a safe and reliable framework for the population. Although the ISILS footprint has not been as marked as in Iraq, there were violent clashes in Tripoli in the north and in Arsal at the border with Syria by October, as well as between the Lebanese army and Sunni groupings linked to the ISIL and the Al-Nusra frontline of Al-Qaeda. Lebanon has now been without President since May due to the rivalry between Hizbollah-supported parties and the Sunni Muslim «Future Movement». International pressure is important to implement a presidential election and to have a government that respects and respects the country's constitution. The main focus of the government in development policy is education, especially for children in conflict areas. Understandably, schools in Lebanon do not have the capacity or resources to receive the large number of children coming. According to figures from the UN High Commissioner for Refugees, in 2014, Lebanon has been able to provide schooling for about 90 000 Syrian refugee children, while there are still about 385,000 who do not receive schooling. In cooperation with UNHCR, including: Save the Children and the Refugee Aid, established Lebanese authorities in May this year a strategy to build schools with the necessary equipment. The goal is to provide more than 400,000 children with education by 2016. It is important to follow up the work of ensuring schooling in order to avoid generations losing both education and the future. As one of the largest contributors to Syria and the countries in the region, Norway is working closely with other countries contributing, both financially and with a view to achieving political results. Norway is already accepting a large proportion of Syrian refugees. The government has made changes to the aid facility, where increased funds are released and directed towards humanitarian assistance and emergency assistance to respond to the increased need. Overall, support to Syria and neighbouring countries in 2014 is now close to 1 billion, to which the Minister for Foreign Affairs was involved. kr – with addition of approximately 220 million. humanitarian aid to Iraq. The funds go primarily to shelters, water and sanitary equipment, food aid, emergency health and education. On 1 December, the World Food Programme announced that because of the lack of funding, they had to suspend the supply of food vouchers to 1.7 million refugees. It was then important that the government immediately increased the Norwegian contribution by 70 million. kr, earmarked for this work. So Norway contributes significantly, and takes a significant responsibility. Through the budgetary agreement, the number of quota refugees has increased by 500, so that Norway will receive more than 2000 next year. In addition, humanitarian efforts are increased by 424 million. NOK to be able to respond quickly. In a picture, Lebanon has become a lifeboat for Syrian refugees, but it is a crowded lifeboat and a great risk that it will be able to bend and sink. In addition to deploying aid in Lebanon, Jordan and neighbouring countries, there is an urgent need for a political solution in Syria. It is therefore critical that the UN's efforts in Syria succeed and that the proposed Armistice Agreement be implemented in a short time. The success of international forces in the fight against ISIL is also essential. Only if the civil war in Syria ends can real incentives for a safe return of refugees from Lebanon be put in place. I am therefore pleased that the Minister for Foreign Affairs is showing action and that he shares the impatience in the work for peace and stability in this region.
ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-15.ana.ud2571e425	Let me also start by thanking the interpellant for addressing an important issue. But there is no solution to bringing some of these refugees to Europe. The goal must be to get temporary help there, so that they can travel back. So the interpellant claims that there is no room in neighbouring countries, in the neighbourhoods. He had flown over two of the countries and found out that here they might be. Well, I don't think that's a good solution either. I think we should try to get them back, so that they can build their own country. The goal of each refugee must be to return to his own country and contribute to building up — not to bring out the most resource-intensive in order not to build up. At least it's my vision. I don't think I'm that small either with the rest of the Norwegian population. I take the responsibility to say that I think there are many who share that point of view that I am speaking to, but as it is very little politically correct to say. Nor can I hear the cries of the Labour Party rapporteurs in the local government around: Yes, let us accept refugees! It is easy to be tall and dark on the platform and say that Norway is not going to pay for it itself, and believe that it is the taxpayers and others who are going to put up and pay shelter for those who come to this country. When they come to this country, there are actually some appliances out there that are going to do something for them – there are some school classes to be set up, there are different institutions that need to be strengthened. This costs money, and it goes beyond the well-being of all of us. I dare say. In fact, there is well-being in Norway. It affects pensions, it affects all other welfare schemes in Norway, if we receive more and more refugees. In addition, this has created instability in Europe. More and more countries in Europe are experiencing instability by receiving more and more refugees. Neither is this the solution – I think we all really see that this is not the solution. Then we have to find a solution that's better, that's to help them in the neighbourhoods, so that they can go back. That's the solution. So instead of taking 500 refugees to Europe, you can help 14,000 refugees in the neighbourhoods – on average. Is it, then, merciful to take some refugees to Europe, or is it merciful to help wherever possible, in the neighbourhoods? We have to do both, you say. If you're going to do both, you're going to help everyone. Is it not best to help as much as possible, or is it best to pursue symbol politics and take someone to Europe and say: Look how good we are, how merciful we are, how moral we are! No, the very best thing is to help them where they are, so we can help more people. Then they can travel back the day it is possible to travel back – and build up their own country. There have been refugee disasters in Europe for many years – many decades, many hundreds of years. The goal has always been to travel back and build up their own country. It must be the goal of refugees – whether they come from Iraq, from Syria, whether they are in Lebanon or elsewhere. Yes, we'll help. The Minister of Foreign Affairs has said that we have contributed 1 billion. kr. to different applications. For my part, we have to contribute more, but the point is: we have to help them where they are, so that they can travel back and build their own country – and then also take responsibility for their own situation.
ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-15.ana.ud2571e448	Five to six years ago, I had the joy of meeting Amin Malouf, one of the greatest writers of our time, in my view, and probably the most famous Lebanese for us in the West. His life sums up the kind of country that Lebanon is in many ways. He was born in a small village, he spoke Arabic as his native tongue, but he went to a French school. He is born in a country where Islam is the dominant religion, but he himself is a Christian. He is a fugitive, because he was an editor of a profiled newspaper, and in 1975 he was forced to flee the country and has since bid in France. He has published literature based not least on migration and the meeting between religions and cultures in the Middle East, for example. the book ‘The Crusades seen on the Arab side’. I would recommend that anyone who understands the Middle East read it. On Lebanon’s day of independence, November 22, preschool children in the city of Arsal, north of Lebanon, were asked to sign the flag. Many draw the flag of ISIL and Jabhat al-Nusra. Arsal is a border town and is in a way the epicenter of Lebanon’s meeting with ISIL – yet it is speaking – and the threat from ISIL can be felt directly in parts of Lebanon. But Lebanon's meeting with what is now happening in the Middle East, is happening right now, however, most strongly through the refugee situation. Lebanon has a 360 km long border with Syria. As of December 9, there were over 1.1 million officially registered Syrian refugees. Everyone calculates that the real number is significantly higher because many refugees don't register. About a quarter of Lebanon's population are no Syrian refugees. But the challenges are not only in numbers and quantities, but also in other areas: Lebanon has not joined the Refugee Convention, and they have no official refugee camp, since they will not be a party to the war in Syria. As a result, Syrian refugees do not have the rights normally granted to refugees under international conventions. They are totally dependent on unofficial refugee leaders, on support from the local population and various relief organisations. And research shows that as much as 20 pst. of the people of Lebanon have a Syrian refugee living at home. The problem is that very many people are unable to pay for themselves, because the help comes mainly through coupons and not through cash, which leads to serious dissatisfaction and conflicts associated with it, of course. In addition, there is a major problem in the labour market in Lebanon, because very many refugees want to work. There is a large informal labour market, with lower wages, which pushes Lebanese out of the labour market. So the Syrian refugee situation in Lebanon is not only very pressured in numbers, but it also threatens to increase the already strong, underlying conflicts that exist in Lebanese society, and threatens stability there. Ideas now also show that a number in Lebanon will have stricter border control and fuller stoppages in the influx of refugees to Lebanon. It is quite simply that the refugee situation we are now seeing in Lebanon is a threat to both the stability we have appreciated in Lebanon over the last few years, and that is what Lebanon is – a very important country with a positive role in the Middle East in many ways. The reasoning that we are helping more people in Lebanon than in Norway is an intellectual card house. It would come together if you breath on it – or if one looks marginally into the situation in Lebanon. So what should we do? First of all, we can contribute even more financially. I am very pleased that the government has increased humanitarian support in the budget for next year. SV has proposed an extra billion, because 2014 is the year since World War II that there are most refugees and internally displaced persons in the world, and an extreme situation requires an extraordinary response from Norway and other countries. Secondly, we can accept more, as SV has proposed, which will be discussed later today. Now we know that a lot of municipalities – not least big cities – have decided that they are willing to accept some more, not least those who need it the most. It is also the appeal from Amnesty and other organizations internationally to all governments: accept some of those who really need the most – disabled children, those who are really hurt. In addition, I found it interesting to hear the Foreign Minister's reply to Eirik Sivertsen's invitation to take the lead in a European effort, which I believe will be a very wise and important initiative from the government.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-15.ana.ud2571e549	I assume that the respective parties themselves explain their tax and tax arrangements for 2015, and I will therefore concentrate in my article on the government and the Right proposal for taxes and taxes. For the right, it is important to work towards a lower level of taxation in order to make Norway more robust to meet the challenges of the future, not least during a transition period where the growth momentum of the oil sector is expected to deteriorate in the future and the need for new jobs, secure jobs is increasing. Values must be created before they can be distributed. This is an important starting point for the government's fiscal policy and in line with both the cooperation agreement between the government parties and the Christian People's Party and the Left and Sundvolden Declaration. We believe that we have provided a solution in line with this through the government's original proposal and the budgetary agreement that was concluded between the four cooperation parties on 21 November 2015. The main objective of the government's fiscal policy is to finance common resources as efficiently as possible. Taxes and charges should be designed to maximise the use of resources. The combination of broad tax bases, low tax rates and tax equal treatment of industries, businesses and investments will contribute to this. These guidelines have been leading the way in the last two decades, and this is also the basis of the government’s tax regime. The government aims to reduce the overall level of taxation in order to spread power, increase value creation and give greater freedom to families and individuals. The world has become more global. Both capital and people move on simpler and faster than before. This gives some leads to a national tax debate. Firstly, it is important to maintain the desire of the Nordics to invest in their own country. That is why we need to develop a tax system that makes this more attractive. Secondly, we need to reduce the scope of action for international tax adaptation and the erosion of the Norwegian tax base. The government is working for a more growth-enhancing and simpler tax system. Last week, Norway’s Bank reduced its interest rate by 0.25 percentage points to 1.25pst. The oil price has fallen sharply, and the growth in the Norwegian economy has weakened. Uncertainty about future developments is high. This was also thoroughly highlighted in the financial debate on the state budget for 2015, which we had in Stortinget on 1 December. The activity in the petroleum industry is decreasing, causing ring-effects to the economy. It is important that the government provides a budget and a tax package that responds to these challenges, responding to the need for safe jobs and the need for new jobs. Then we need to encourage investments in the future. This is why government’s commitment to growth-enhancing areas such as knowledge, research and development, transport and other infrastructures is important. The government is solving important tasks today and equips Norway for the future. In the tax system, we provide a policy to help strengthen growth capacity in the future. We will organise our tax policy in a way that will make it more attractive to work, save, invest and act environmentally friendly. The government's initial draft budget for 2015 contained new tax reductions of nearly 8.3 billion. kr bn and SEK 6.9 bn. I have been booked. After the budgetary agreement, these figures have changed slightly. One of the main steps in the tax regime is the reduction in property taxes. Reduce the rate by 1.0 pst. to 0,85 pst. and the bottom deduction is increased from 1 million. SEK to 1.2 million. kr per person and from 2,0 mill. NOK 2.4 million. kr for married couples. In order to reduce the tax favorisation of property investment, the secondary and commercial real estate valuation discount is set at 30 pst. There are also large tax reliefs on income. The minimum deduction is increased for wages, salaries, social security and pensions. Less will pay top taxes as the entry point increases. Strengthening the working line. In addition to the tax changes, it is also proposed to cut and convert a number of charges. Among other things, there is considerable relief on the vehicle by reducing the average re-registration fee by 35 pst. The budgetary agreement makes a number of other changes to promote more environmentally friendly behaviour, such as: a lower charge on hybrid cars by eliminating the lowest achievable one-off charge and increasing the deduction of the weight component of the one-off charge to 26 pst. for plug-in hybrid vehicles. Increased turnover requirements for biofuels for road transport, half the user charge for bioethanol and removal of the user charge on biodiesel – to mention some of the grips made towards a green tax shift. It is gratifying that the entry point for the rent tax is increased from 5 to 10 VAT, as promised in the Sundvolden Declaration, thus contributing to greater profitability for renewable energy production in small-scale power plants across the country. Furthermore, it is also positive that the government signals better depreciation rules for wind turbines in order to stimulate investment in Norway under the common electricity licence market with Sweden. As is known, half of this scheme is financed by Norwegian consumers, and at that time at least half of the investments in new renewable energy production should take place here in Norway. There has been a lot of discussion about the taxes on wealth and the changes proposed. For the government and for the right, it is important to implement reductions in this tax. This is important because: Firstly, the property tax is capped by Norwegian capital companies that could have been invested in safe jobs. Secondly, the taxes on wealth are hostile to entrepreneurs. There will be no more business start-ups, value creation and employment of demanding a high special-purpose tax on Norwegian jobs and then giving some of that in different innovation packages afterwards. Thirdly, property taxes contribute to more short-term ownership than long-term ownership. Fourth, the wealth tax weakens the ability to strengthen diversity and local ownership. Fifthly, the wealth tax penalises normal savings. Sixth, property taxes have to be paid regardless of whether the enterprise is running in profit or in deficit and reduce the ability of Norwegian enterprises to invest in innovation, development and new jobs. For the seventh, the property tax favours foreign interests and gives Norwegian-owned jobs a competitive disadvantage because the property tax is levied only on Norwegian citizens, not on foreign owners. I find it difficult to find one single good argument as to why we should favour foreign owners at the expense of Norwegian owners to Norwegian companies and jobs. Why should a Chinese owner, a British owner or other foreign owners be favored over a Norwegian owner? We welcome foreign investors to invest in Norway, but this should take place within a framework of competition neutrality, and the wealth tax is distorting competition in favour of foreign investors. That the wealth tax is a problem can be illustrated by a notice in NRK Telemark published on 17 November 2014. Under the heading ‘Norwegian investors fail the industry’, it appears that no Norwegian investors have been willing to invest in new magnesium factories on the island of Here Island in Porsgrund. So far, the magnesium producer SilMag has only foreign investors in his back. In an entry at NRK Telemark the following day, a regional investor in Grenland found that the framework conditions were too bad and that the property tax had to be removed in order to make it interesting for him to invest. We depend on both the ability to invest and the willingness to invest to secure existing jobs and create new jobs. Right wants to make it more attractive to invest and thus facilitate growth at a time that will be more demanding in the future, where the growth momentum of the petroleum sector into the economy as such will be weaker. An effective and effective tax regime is an important framework for achieving this. Right-wing efforts to create a more growth-enhancing and simpler tax system. It is worth working, saving and investing, and it is worth paying to be environmentally friendly. Such a tax policy will have a positive impact on value creation, competitiveness and income over time. Right wants to develop new ideas and better solutions to meet the challenges Norway has today and will have in the future. Tax policy increases private sector growth and strengthens Norwegian private ownership. More profitability in business creates safe jobs. Finally, I would like to thank the members of the Committee on Finance for a good cooperation on the tax regime for 2015.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-15.ana.ud2571e676	Two key features of the Norwegian welfare model are high participation in employment and a tax system that finances our common well-being. The tax system therefore needs to be set up in a way that contributes to value creation, and jobs and revenues adapted to the scale and quality of the public services we want. In addition, the tax system shall contribute to reducing societal differences and shifting consumption towards a sustainable direction. For the Workers' Party, it is important to have a tax system that is effective, simple, predictable, fair and redistributive. This requires companies to have good and predictable framework conditions, to encourage employees to work, to contribute to the fissure layer, and to carry the heaviest burden with the strongest back. The tax system should be broad-based and low rates – minimal exceptions and gaps – and should be progressive. Norway has bigger challenges than the country's richest paying too much tax, and it's hard to imagine that we're solving some of the challenges of the richest paying less tax. The government's largest single investment, cut in wealth taxes, is a low-efficiency, low-performance way of strengthening Norwegian business, but it is very accurate to increase differences. The government's own calculations show that the 15 pst. The richest in the country receives more than 90 pst. of the tax base. In the 2014 draft budget, the government showed that they would take Norway in a direction of increasing disparities. In 2015 they take much longer steps in that direction. With the government's proposal, they would have 5 pst. The richest of us get almost half of the total tax, while 85 pst. of those with the lowest wealth would get at the bottom of 40 pst. This is in stark contrast to the promises of tax cuts to most people. Since the government joined, the tax rate for the individual family with average income, with no wealth and with two children in kindergarten is significantly less than the increase in the family's childcare bill with the government's total increase in child care prices. In order to finance tax cuts to the richest of the country, the government proposed in its draft budget cuts for the unemployed, sick, disabled and disabled. This has touched on broadly anchored values in Norway. The analysis seems to be that the richest are motivated by more, while the poorest are motivated by less. The polls say theirs. The agreement in Stortinget has made the impression of the proposals somewhat better. The worst cuts of the weakest groups are fixed, and the richest pay a little bit more in taxes. It's nice how these things relate. However, we in the Labour Party are in agreement with the parliamentary leader in Right, Trond Helleland, who, at the time of the conciliation, found that the main actions are firm, i.e. those that have the most, get more. The deal goes 27 pst. of the tax on wealth of the country’s 0,1 pst. Richest. Did anyone say "people most"? This is a distribution profile that completely breaks down with the key characteristics of what we can call the Norwegian model. Had these figures been anonymised and presented to an international panel, we would have guessed that it was Norway we talked about. It is strange that the government is unable to account well for its largest single effort in the budget. Someone might call it a little faint. It is not capable of probating that the proposed grips promote growth, as it claims. On the contrary, they themselves write in the Ministry of Finance’s tax proposal: ‘There is no basis for budgeting with dynamic effects in 2015 of the proposals for tax changes. ‘If you go to the country’s leading experts in the field, Professor Jarle Møen of the NHH says that most economists do not believe that taxes on wealth tax give any particular growth, it is very difficult to quantify the effect of cuts in wealth tax, and then you are left with some theoretical reasoning. Last week we received a report from the OECD, where they write that economic inequality has a negative impact on economic growth, while redistributive policies can contribute to growth. So the government's arrangement is in conflict with the technical recommendations. The government's proposals for tax arrangements lack anchoring both in the people and in research, and appear to be purely ideologically justified. The government has proposed that legitimacy and trust in the tax system be at stake. It's serious. The government's meeting with the voters has from the outside of this harvest appeared to be quite comical when one tries to explain tax cuts without professional anchoring or explains to an unadulterated mother that it is for her own good that she gets large cuts in income. Funny, but not funny. Funny it was only when the government and the conciliators were going to play the answer about who was going to be Aunt Pose. Entertainment attitude is the name of the debut album of a well-known Norwegian music group. Unfortunately, it is not possible to transfer this title to the debut budget to the Right and the Advance Party. I don't know what was the plan of the government, but for the voters and commentators in the press, this government has appeared more like a government that maintains, than a government with attitudes. The Labour Party has made choices to maintain the tax level from 2013. This implies that the Workers’ Party proposes for 2015 to restore the overall level of taxation imposed by the government of Stoltenberg II for eight years. The allegation made by Minister of Finance Jensen that taxes increased each year with the Workers' Party in government is demonstrably wrong. There is no phase at the perfect tax level, but it is heavy that 350,000 new jobs were created – two out of three outside the public sector – and thousands of enterprises in the period 2005–2013, all with unchanged tax levels. And it was a progressive tax system. In the World Bank’s Ease of Doing Business index, Norway is the sixth best country to operate in. Despite the overall level of taxation in the upper reaches of OECD countries, we have set up the tax system in a way that contributes to efficiency and value creation. With the previous tax reform in 1992, we increased the tax base and reduced the rates. The company tax rates were reduced from more than 50 pst. to 28 pst. The result was a success. Many have changed since 1992. At that time we had one of the lowest corporate taxes in the OECD, today we're above average. Capital is moving faster between countries, and the tax rates in Norway are considerably higher than in the countries around us. This gives Norwegian companies strong motivation to move investment out of the country. The government of Stoltenberg II took the first steps to meet these challenges. In the business growth package, Minister of Finance Sigbjørn Johnnsen announced in May 2013 that the corporate tax rate would be reduced and that it would be taken to close tax gaps and limit unwanted adjustments. The Scheel Committee recommends further reduction of corporation tax and, in order to avoid major adjustments, the rate of income tax is required to follow. The bill could be almost 100 billion. kr. If we are to be able to maintain today's welfare level in Norway, the income of the country must be about the same level as it is today. Then we're talking about a big tax conversion of the Norwegian tax system. A review of the tax system must also help to close gaps in the tax system currently used by multinational companies. These companies exploit differences in tax rates between countries by raising the proportion of the group in high tax countries to finance other parts of the group, while equity is concentrated in low tax countries. This is often called aggressive tax planning. Others call it deliberately to avoid taxes. Regardless of the label, there is a very uneven competition between local enterprises and local businesses on the one hand and multinational companies on the other. For those who claim to have the woes of Norwegian businesses and well as one of their strongest priorities, this should be a major factor. Work on country-by-country reporting cannot wait three more years, as the government and Jensen say. The Workers’ Party does not propose to reintroduce the inheritance tax according to the model in force in 2013 and to compensate for this, the Workers’ Party proposes that the property tax be increased so that the total tax on wealth is approximately as in 2013. By reducing the tax advantage of secondary housing and commercial property, we are now following up previous steps to reduce the difference in taxation between different property items, in line with the recommendation of an overall economic professional environment. This will make it less lucrative than in the past to invest in secondary housing and commercial property rather than to invest in, for example, a machine park. This will also contribute to a more effective tax system. ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-15.ana.ud2571e1108	Let me first record the SV's suggestions in the setting. In 1927, U.S. Supreme Judge Oliver Wendell Holmes wrote the following in a judgment: «It is true, as indicated in the last quoted case, that every expression of money for an act is a discount to the extent of the payment required, but that which in its immediacy is a discouraging may be part of an invitation when seen in its organic connection with the whole. Taxes are what we pay for civilized society.» Here Holmes touches on a dimension at the tax debate that is often absent both in Norwegian public and here in Stortinget. First, taxes and taxes are not just for the purpose of providing income to the state and municipalities, and, second, taxes and taxes are part of a whole that makes Norway a better country to live in than most other countries. When one listens to the Right and Advance Party in this debate and in other discussions on tax, one should think that tax was almost a theft and something one should at best reluctantly accept. Their own view of the limited legitimacy of the tax itself is used as an argument to reduce taxes as much as possible. The idea is that money earned exclusively is own money, and that any taxation involves a confiscation that can only be regarded as a necessary evil. So when an industrialist, for example, reaches having large profits in its business, it is considered by the right to be totally unreasonable that these are values on which particular taxes are to be taxed. He has created the values, as they say. Is that right? Who is creating the values in Norway? Does not both business and private people serve well on infrastructure, on roads and on rail, which allows goods to be transported to the market? Does not a good welfare offer, good schools, and kindergartens allow families to live where values are created? Is not the community management of the natural resources used a prerequisite for production? And does not Norwegian wage and tax levels contribute to the return of efficient, competent labour, so that more values are created and more wealth spread across the whole population? Norway can never compete only at pay level. Even without the oil, we would be a country with a pay level well above other countries that produce many of the same goods we live on. What makes us competitive is innovation, quality and productivity. The prerequisites for this are public research effort, welfare schemes, quality at all stages. It doesn't give us tax cuts. We know this, because it's tried. The U.S. once had a tax system that functioned somewhat redistributive. It was the end of the 1980s. The idea that increasing wealth to the richest would benefit everyone else because more jobs would be "created" – I say created in quotes because everyone really knows that there are supply chains that create values, not shareholders – was tested to the full. Then we can ask the even American family if they think the real decline in wages over the last decades has been something to boast about. The families who have experienced a real wage cut may not agree. The leaders of the United States have experienced a multiple of their income, a much greater increase than the actual growth in profits in businesses. The only thing that happened when taxes on the highest wealth and income went down was that a few people became much richer. Therefore, SV wants a beneficial tax system that benefits most people and creates a more fair society. Our tax line combines the cut in income tax for most people with environmental taxes. That's how we contribute to a more environmentally friendly society, without getting a biased social profile. We suggest that those who have the most should contribute more. That's why we're proposing a new step 3 in the top tax on revenues over 1.5 million. kr. We propose to make the wealth tax progressive, so that with the large wealth they pay a higher rate, and we propose to end what is actually subsidising the financial industry, and that government itself in the national budget calls under-taxation, by introducing VAT on financial services. Last week, a report from the OECD showed how economic differences are generating lower growth in the economy. While the government parties have married an out-of-date idea that tax cuts to the richest benefit us all, more and more evidence emerges that smaller differences provide better communities. And while government parties use the need to finance welfare in the future as an argument for cutting welfare systems, the solution is really right in front of them: smaller differences will give us better advice on the welfare of the future. We can make ourselves less dependent on oil and at the same time create a society where more people feel better. But something tells me that it wasn't the welfare of the future that stood out for the sake of taking when the government decided to cut dramatically the taxes of those who have the most.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-15.ana.ud2571e1178	Today’s debate confirms that the government’s main political project is to continue the exceptional growth period in Norway over the last few years. The question is how long it is wise policy to increase consumption, use more oil money and increase debt to increase growth. The Greens believe that this is an increasingly poor economic policy which is becoming less and less forward-looking. Last week many, to whom several references have been made, heard the lecture by French economist Thomas Piketty in the University's auditorium. One of his points, which has been little up in the debate, is that economic growth is generally decreasing in rich countries. More and more economists believe that growth will continue to fall, and there is an estimate of growth of 1.2 pst. or less in the West towards 2100. Population growth is falling, and productivity growth is decreasing because more and more people are going to work in professions that cannot be automated much more, and because tasks that can be robotized have already become so. The probability is increasing that economic growth will continue to slow down, and traditional economic thinking means that this is dangerous. The question is what we do if declining growth is an increasingly necessary result, both of earth's resource and climate challenges and of technological and demographic developments. For the Green Party, this means that we must look for a new economic policy, while the government is doing the opposite. They still have higher economic growth for the world’s richest country as their biggest political project, and they increase the focus on the old growth recipe – almost on autopilot. Last week, Norway’s Bank reduced its interest rate to 1.25 pst. to stimulate growth. ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-15.ana.ud2571e1266	The Norwegian economy is changing. Lower demand from the oil industry will require Norwegian enterprises and Norwegian labour force to target other sectors. In such a situation, the tax system must also stimulate transitions and contribute to enhancing the growth capacity of the economy. Business activities in Norway are to be profitable and more attractive to work. There is therefore a need for growth-enhancing tax relief. The budget agreement reduced taxes by 5.2 billion. kr from 2014 to 2015. This is less than the government suggested, but still considerable relief. Since the government took over in the autumn of 2013, the level of taxation has been reduced by 12.5 billion. kr. The taxes on wealth make it less profitable to save and weaken private Norwegian ownership. In addition, different valuations of property contribute to distorting the investment from business to real estate. It reduces the total yield. Following the budgetary agreement, the rate of property tax is reduced to 0,85 pst. and the bottom deduction increased to 1,2 million. kr. At the same time, the valuation discount for secondary and commercial dwellings is reduced to 30 pst. Thus, over two years we have reduced the adverse effects of property taxes. About 90 000 people with ordinary, small assets are exempt from property taxes in 2015, and in the further work on the property tax, the government will also assess a model of exemptions for so-called working capital, in line with the budgetary agreement. The government's draft budget stressed that broad groups of the population should benefit from tax relief. I am pleased that the budgetary agreement has also provided room for lower taxes for large groups of the population. 99 pst. the taxpayers receive relief or unchanged tax. About 28 000 fewer will pay top taxes, and significant reductions in minimum deductions are made to both employees and old-age pensioners. The average relief for employees is NOK 1 200, while the average age pensioners receive NOK 1 600 less tax. The greatest relief in percent of gross income goes to the tenth of the population with the lowest gross income. The transitional arrangements for non-pensioners have been improved in the budgetary agreement, so as to facilitate the transition to new rules for persons with particularly large debts. In the State budget, the government proposed significant improvements in car charges. The re-registration fee has been proposed to be reduced by 35 p.m. and other vehicle and fuel charges were also proposed. The budget agreement gives us greater relief in car charges than the government proposed. This is because we agreed to reduce the charges on environmentally friendly vehicles and fuels. I am pleased that there will be a tax relief that reduces costs both for normal drivers and for the transport industry, while facilitating more environmentally friendly choices. In the budget agreement, we also agreed that the Left and Christian People's Party would participate in the further review of vehicle and fuel charges, and that the parties would jointly submit a agreed agreement on future taxes. In addition, the government will continue to work for tax relief and for the tax system to contribute to more efficient resource use, improve conditions for Norwegian business and stimulate more environmentally friendly choices. In the follow-up to the Scheel Committee, we will work in line with the Sundvollen Declaration to achieve a more competitive tax system that can promote profitable investments and provide high value creation. In particular, this is becoming important as a result of lower demand from the oil industry in the years to come.

We in the Committee understand that it is the customs office who knows when serious smuggling is expected and agrees with the fact that it is right to base control. We got a demonstration of how they work with drug dogs. We received a review of the station's work. We got to see how they work on car scanning, and how they interpret the scanning images. ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-15.ana.ud2571e1559	The Committee has conscientiously examined the report of the National Audit Office, and we have also been on an inspection at the customs office of Swinesund to find out how the department organises its work and what tools it has to carry out its mission. On this basis, the Committee has drawn its conclusions, which in this regard only partially coincide with the conclusions of the Riksaudition. The National Audit Office study is both thorough and relevant, and in many areas both the Customs Service, the National Audit Office and the Committee agree that there are weaknesses in border control, but not in all areas. The usual thing is that the Committee largely shares the comments and recommendations of the National Audit Office, but I think it is a great strength for the authority of the Supervisory and Constitutional Committee that we make our own, independent and conscientious assessments, and we have done so in this matter. We note that the customs service does not share the Riksrevision's view that serious infringements are not a high priority. Based on the information provided to the Committee, in particular with regard to the information we received on the inspection, our assessment is that the resource priority in customs is satisfactory. We note that the Riksrevision believes that seizures for several types of currency and drugs are low, which is quite correct when compared to the total amount in circulation in Norway. At the same time, we note that the 2013 seizures were higher than for our neighbouring Nordic countries in terms of the vast majority of drugs. We note the Riksrevision's remark that the crew is slightly adapted to increased traffic during weekends and holidays, but we also note that the customs department believes that there are purely control reasons on the basis of which the manning was carried out throughout the year. The Committee also agrees that there must be control professional considerations leading to the manning situation and the watch plan. On weekends and holidays, there are mostly ordinary people crossing the borders, not trailers with a lot of goods. In my opinion, it is a good thing in itself that cross-border crossings go as smoothly as possible for ordinary people. Total control of ordinary citizens is not possible and definitely not desirable. We also note that the study shows that there are weaknesses in ICT, physical control facilities at some border crossings, the use of mobile scanners and tip channels. The Committee agrees that these are weaknesses and we ask for them to be followed up. In sum, I have the impression that this report is useful for the Customs and Fee Directorate in many areas, although the Directorate does not agree with all of the Riksrevision's comments. Otherwise, it may be mentioned that this is a unanimous vote by the Committee on Control and Constitutional Affairs, and we support the rapporteur's good report.

Furthermore, I refer to the good comments made by the rapporteur.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-15.ana.ud2571e1634	The situation for the oil industry is dramatic. In the last six months we have received more and more notices of termination, especially in the supplier industry. There are 10,000 fewer people this year and another 10,000 to 15,000 who have to go next year. The supply industry employs 136 000 people. Over 40 pst. of these jobs located along the coast, from West Agder to Hordaland. We now see that the unemployment rate in West Agder, Rogaland and Hordaland has increased by 14 pst. last year, while the increase for the rest of the country was 2 pst. The Labour Party is deeply concerned about the situation. It's about people who lose their jobs, an important industry that loses competence and capacity, and impaired value creation. The oil industry is a cyclical industry, and we have gone through both ups and downs in all the years we have had oil business in Norway. While the previous downstrokes were largely about fluctuations in the oil price, we now have several factors that work in the same direction. Over many years of increased activity and high oil prices, the level of costs has increased significantly in both oil companies and the supply industry. On the new construction side, our offshore shipyard has had problems with its competitiveness against Asian shipyards. At the same time, we see that the activity of operation, maintenance and modifications has fallen sharply. In addition to the need for cost reductions, we see that the capital discipline of oil companies itself contributes to the reduction of activity. In order to satisfy shareholders’ short-term wishes for returns, investments in future value creation are reduced. The third factor that drives down the activity is falling oil prices. The situation in the oil market is dramatic, and it doesn't seem like we're getting a normalization of the supply/demand ratio in the next few years. Full oil stocks are reported worldwide, which means that it will take time for increased demand or reduced production to determine price. The overall goal of Norwegian petroleum policy is to maximise the values and the best possible value creation of our oil and gas resources as a nation. That's how far we've done well. We have high extraction rates, low emissions, and we have managed to build a large national supplier industry. In a troubled time that is now, it is important that we have a complete look at what good resource management is. At my discretion, it is about managing natural resources, investments and the competence and capacity of all the 250,000 people who work in connection with the industry. Ever since we received the so-called ten oil bids in 1971, such an overall perspective has been used as a basis for our petroleum policy. For this reason, cyclic downturns have also been met with political action to mitigate negative impacts and provide the basis for good industry development. The introduction of the NORSOK standard, the establishment of the Konkraft cooperative body, where the oil industry meets both the supplier industry and trade movement, and the establishment of INTSOK, in order to strengthen the international position of the supplier industry, are all examples of actions that have been initiated in bad times. Low oil prices, slowing down investments and high costs are affecting all oil producing countries to a greater or lesser extent. What separates us from other countries is our ability to pull the load together in bad times. The Norwegian model, where businesses, trade unions and authorities sit down and find solutions, is a competitive advantage in times like this. The working party wants uniform and high value creation on a Norwegian base. It doesn't come of itself. Now, when it cancels out, we have to see what we can do to dampen the fall in activity. As politicians, we cannot override the markets, and we should not hold up unprofitable projects, but we must nevertheless take seriously the challenge we are facing. During the autumn, the working party has submitted several proposals for action to address the situation. With too many field developments, we have experienced that large contracts are won by foreign shipyards with low bids, but that the final result will be major delays and excessive cost growth during the construction period. As much of the cost is written off on the tax, the community is most exposed to the impact of poor processing and poor contract strategies in the projects. It is a fact that construction at Norwegian workshops is much less affected by delays and running costs. We have therefore proposed that, in addition to the excellent work they do in assessing the petroleum industry in the development and operation plan, the Oil Directorate should also be set up with the competence to assess the strategies for implementing the development. In this way, we can reduce risk and improve resource control and use. We believe that the government's tightening of the permitting legislation was wrong, and that this policy is particularly negative for the supplier industry now. For example, in the case of the courts, this means that they have an obligation to pay for the first two months after the permit has entered into force. This is because workers in 2- to 4-offshore rotation work only 10 days per month. Thus, the purpose of the law on permitting, i.e. to enable the employer to overcome a temporary order failure without losing the firm’s competence, is not respected for this sector. It can also be said for other parts of the supplier industry. Among other things, the background is that both the NHO and the LO require the employer’s pay obligation to be reduced from 20 to 10 days. Many have expressed concern that a fall in activity is at the expense of necessary maintenance and safety. The report of the Petroleum Authority on the level of risk in Norwegian petroleum activities 2013 on maintenance states: ‘The figures from 2010 to 2013 show no significant improvement related to maintenance management. For production devices, the total amount of corrective maintenance outstanding and release for preventive maintenance in 2013 is at the same level as in 2012. However, the level of corrective maintenance outstanding in 2013 is significantly higher than for 2010 and 2011. Outstanding corrective maintenance of the quantities reported will contribute to the risk in itself.» There are large numbers, and there is a lot of work that needs to be done sooner or later. The working party considers that the suspension should be reduced and that the available capacity can be put into operation. I am amazed that the proposal that the government makes sure that the activity case does not go at the expense of maintenance and safety was voted down by the majority of the population. When the kerosene message arrived in 2011, a lack of rigging was reported on the Norwegian base. This had a significant negative impact on the cost level, it was said. Our government took the initiative to remedy this. Now the situation is that riggers are in storage, and contracts are suspended. During 2014, five drilling devices have left the socket and several will follow it. During 2015 and 2016, a further 10 devices will become available. At the same time, we are told that there are many wells on the Norwegian pedestal that are abandoned, but that are only temporarily studied. It is uncertain how many wells there are that should be studied permanently. Norwegian oil and gas are talking about 3,000 wells per day. The Petroleum Authority has carried out a study showing that out of 282 temporarily abandoned wells, 132 issues related to barrier to emissions are issues. The working party believes that we can tackle this now when we have free rigging capacity. This is work that needs to be done sooner or later, and in this way we increase the safety of the base while employing free rigging capacity and preventing the rigging from leaving the Norwegian base. It is sad that this idea meets with great resistance from the government parties, but the more pleased that Statoil could tell on Friday that they have taken back the rig Songa Drum from suspension to new missions with just well-plugging. The Norwegian supplier industry is a highly skilled industry. These are competences developed and maintained through activity. Falling activity also leads to a deterioration in skills and capacity. We believe that in such a situation, a job should be done to identify the situation now, see what lies ahead of us in terms of skills needs, and see whether – and in the case of how – the competence of the supplier industry can be further developed, including in areas other than petroleum activities. The international oil industry is facing tough demands for dividends from its shareholders. We are now in an era of petroleum activity where large investments are required to replace production after each phase. When companies reduce investments in exploration and expansion to have money for dividends, we have a big problem. This can lead to time-critical projects not being realised, and we get a lower use of resources than is possible. We expect companies that receive exploration and extraction permits on a Norwegian base to develop profitable projects in general – not just the super-wage ones. As a majority in Statoil, the Council of State also has a role in the dividend policy of this company. I believe it would be natural for the Council of State to discuss in its own dialogue whether the company's ambitious dividend programme can now reduce the company's ability to create values in the future. Can high yields in today's situation conflict with the country's long-term returns target? The Labour Party has no performance attached to the individual proposals and ideas we bring to the square. We do not have all the answers to what should be done, but we are convinced that the problems that we see evolve will not disappear by themselves. We have to admit that the situation is serious. We need to acquire knowledge about the extent of the situation, what threats it brings, but also what opportunities are. It is time for initiatives to be taken to allow us to go through the bad times without losing the necessary skills and capacity, so that the negative impact of the downturn is minimised. So far, the government's and government's answers have been that they follow the situation closely, but the challenges we face cannot be solved from the Tribune. That's why we're asking for action and we're asking, what does the government want to do?

The situation at the time made the road up again difficult and heavy. We had a lot of problems with recruitment, and we had a solid loss of competence. But then one has clearly, after the case that was the last, to use the time rationally and to show that the petroleum industry is an industry for the future. One has built on the competence, and one has taken care of it. This was achieved in close cooperation between authority, industry and trade union movement. 250,000 people are connected to the industry, so there are many families that get their heads and food on the table as a result of their work in this industry. Then I hear the council say we're not going to talk the industry down, because then it can be a self-fulfilling prophecy, but that's not what one is doing here today either. One now wants one to do that one can do to counteract what is happening, and does not end up in the same situation as one was in the past. The Labour Party is concerned that the government is building up cooperation between the authorities, industry and trade unions. If we do that, we'll undermine the Norwegian model. The industry and the trade union movement have been very clear that the government's tightening of the legislation on permitting has further exacerbated the situation. People are told off instead of getting a permit. We were many who had hoped that this government would make about the decision they made when it came to the law of permacy. It hasn't happened, and the serious consequences of that are now being seen when people get laid off. I am also afraid that this situation will make the competence disappear from the industry – in one of the areas where we really have a advantage competitively through the competence we possess. I am also afraid that the youth will once again turn away from the industry and choose other areas of education, so that on the next crossroads we will have problems with recruitment and with regaining competence. When the government in the finance and in the throne says that they're telling us what's going on, and then they're kind of setting a sentence without telling us what's going to come, and what's going to do about this, that's what worries us today: that we have to know how you're going to deal with this. One has to get an overview of the situation, and then find action that can work well in resolving this situation.

The Petroleum Company is generally the hallmark of systematic work to reduce the risk of accidents, both for humans and for the environment. A well-functioning cooperation between responsible actors and a strong monitoring body is and has been a decisive factor in today’s situation. The challenge is to continue with the positive developments we have seen in the last ten years and to facilitate further improvements. Although there are very few accidents at work, deaths and major accidents, there is a potentially high risk of accidents in the petroleum industry. Therefore, it is serious that we now, as a direct consequence of what we are discussing here today, hear from several quarters that the HMS work is suffering from cost cuts. One example is the safety training of emergency personnel. In addition, there are many challenges to maintenance. Our installations are getting older, and the demand for preventive maintenance is increasing. Some of this is HMS-critical. This dilemma rises particularly in bad times in industry. Most of us are certain that one should operate cost-effectively, and there is probably a need to operate more cost-effectively than what we have used to with high oil prices. But we also have to make it crystal clear that we're going to maintain the high HMS and quality requirements. The need to cut costs cannot be met by HMS. In a debate here in the Chamber in November, we discussed this with the Minister of Labour and tried to make it clear whether the government is as supportive of this principle as the Stoltenberg government did. Our ambition was clear: Norway should be world-leading within the HMS in the petroleum sector. This ambition disappeared from the Government’s letter of allocation to the Petroleum Authority for 2014. It leaves the impression after we have discussed this in Stortinget, it should be that it is by chance that this ambition is drawn away. But the Labour Party isn't completely over-exploited about it. I hope that the Minister of Oil and Energy will help to remove the doubts that the government has created among those who have joined the industry, that Council of State Lien can state here today that it is not part of the government's response to the challenges in the petroleum sector, and that the government will not legitimize to ease HMS requirements as part of the cost cut on the socket. The key to high value creation, competitiveness and even employment in the supplier industry is still technological development and even activity, an industry that, with all its jobs, value creation and competences, is very important, especially for the Western world – and therefore also for the rest of the country. The Labour Party expects the government and the bulk of the population to take this difficult situation into account and to take action to prevent the representative Henriksen from calling a ‘crude stop’ in the petroleum industry from doing permanent damage to the supplier industry and the economy otherwise. A very good start will be if Councillor Lien gets his colleague Eriksson to stop cutting the rules.

It can't be that when we're going to accept refugees, it's the least affected by the war that's coming. It also leads me to the second point, which is about the criteria that we're going to choose. Yes, it is true that it has been so that this has been decided by the government before, but now this shows that it is not going well. Norway is a country that is very dependent and very concerned that the United Nations is going to have a great deal of influence. Norway must then be a country that listens to what the United Nations says in the main crises. When the United Nations says this, we can't have a system where the Norwegian big thing comes up and says no, we're leaving this to a Progress Party government. We know the answer to that now. They will say no to as many as possible, but the Storting needs to state that we are going to follow the UN, and that it is those with the greatest needs to come. We've received many before. Someone mentioned Bosnia, and we took 15 000. When it was Kosovo, it was 6,000. We succeeded because we saw that it was necessary to relieve the proximity. It is necessary not only to accept more, but also that we in the Stortinget now determine what principles it is that we should follow. The SV suggests that we should follow the UN recommendations on who needs protection. I have to say I'm surprised at both the Workers' Party, the Center Party, the Christian People's Party and the Left, who are not in it, but who think it's okay that the government should have the opportunity to change this. Finally, this with families: it has not been promoted as a concrete proposal, but the government has discussed seriously that families split up should not come to Norway. If there's something happening in war, it's that families are being split up. It's one of the worst consequences of a war, and some of them are terribly vulnerable. I think that's how the risk of a lost father or son getting along is why the government says no, because that could be one more than the quota.
