ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-15.ana.ud2571e2411	While the North was impatiently waiting for the development of a better network, the Romanians were working on a bomb to make sure we got it. As payment, they received NOK 7 per hour – NOK 7 per hour. It happened this year, in Norway. Two Lithuanians worked 209,5 hours in Norway and earned NOK 3 400 – NOK 16 per hour. These are gross examples of social dumping. Unfortunately, we know that there are so many more. Last year, the Labour Inspectorate carried out 1,800 inspections on labour crime and social dumping. Most supervision is in the construction industry and in the overnight and catering industries. In 2012, more than 200 of the inspections were carried out immediately due to risks to life and health. It reminds us that labour-life crime and social dumping are more than wages, and it should tear down our souls when we hear that people have it like this at work. Overall, Norwegian working life is serious. It is a safe and good working life, with rules developed in common. It's our common working life. We do not accept threats to this working life. We do not accept that anyone will undermine this working life. We do not accept that someone will make our working life their own, with their own rules of play – or lack of it. The fight against crime at work is primarily about this: about humans, about the injustice committed against people. At the same time, if we are to meet the challenge, we need a broader look – a look at the backers, a look at the employers who believe they are threatened by them, a look at the sectors they think they are in danger, a look at the parties who know every day where the shoe prints. Crime is creative, unfortunately often more creative than what we are in this room. Crime can spread like the parasite it is, affecting seriousness and propriety. Therefore, it is offensiveness and forward-lookingness that must affect our job and our attitude. We refuse to lose. Work-life crime is the cause. Social dumping is the consequence. That we get less tax to pay common welfare schemes is the consequence. The fact that serious actors are at risk of uncompetitiveness is the consequence. The question is, can we do anything? We can and must. Let me therefore give praise to the red-green parties, who put the search light on this when they were in government. For example, I think we can all agree with the government's proposals for changes to the Working Environment Act and the Public Health Act on stricter penalties for breaches of these laws. Action against labour-related crime needs to be developed in cooperation and in interaction with social partners. I record that red-green parties are not on the note about it, but it can only be because there was something positive about the government. In addition, it is important that we state that different agencies can cooperate more and better. There we know that there is an important work that has already been started. The government has also provided a tripled effort in terms of coordination and coordination. All these moments are important elements. But it's when it happens together that the force is greater. The fight against crime at work must be multi-disciplinary, multi-territorial and cross-political. With that, I look forward to the government's announced strategy on labour crime and having a constructive tone.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-15.ana.ud2571e2476	I'll be allowed to start by saying that I'm positively surprised at the first post of the representative Heggelund, as he recognizes the importance of social dumping and labour crime. I assume it's allowed to say that. I shall also say it again if the representative would make other posts in which I believe there is a positive input, but I am a little afraid that there will be no mass on my part. I think it's right to take that as a starting point, because this societal problem is so huge. We're talking about a move in our society that can undermine society's working life. It can undermine serious business. It is obviously undermining the working conditions of Norwegian workers today because they are being pressured by the far too low wages, and in addition, there is an estimate of crime, of the black economy, and even of trafficking in human beings. That's why I think it was positive that the rapporteur took the approach he did, by mentioning some examples that we've seen lately on social dumping, and I also want to underline the media's positive role in revealing it. This is no new problem. What's new is that the recognition is obviously as broad as we now got an illustration of. In our time in government, we presented three action plans against social dumping and crime at work, but that's not what the representative Heggelund said, because it broke a little when he came to the change of line. He said that there has been a great deal of agreement on the measures that have been implemented. No, the truth is that that's exactly what it hasn't been. I have stood here and presented some of the measures when I was Minister of Labour, and it was so that the most important and effective measures voted against the Right and the Advance Party. That's the reality that we've seen. That's exactly why I'm glad that the known problems that have been there for a long time now seem to be recognized by the two government parties. Our fight against social dumping has also been a fight against the Right and the Advance Party here in the Stortinget. Although the problem is not new, the parties are involved in the issue in a positive way. The beginning of the process we have now seen is the letter from the LO to the Prime Minister in ours, who received support from the NHO to a large extent — without necessarily complete duplication of action, but to a large extent still. The parties are engaged precisely because they see serious work in Norway being undermined. Then, after a long time, there was a meeting with the parties, where the letter from the LO was followed up in a meeting with the Prime Minister. I think it was Dagbladet who wrote that a country should be happy because one has such social partners that take as much social responsibility as the parties in Norway do. On that basis, and because of the small incident, we found it right to start working thoroughly on the Document 8 proposal that we are dealing with. For our part, this is in the extension of the three action plans we have put forward in the past, and it is the result of the meeting with both the leader of the LO and the head of the NHO, who was invited to participate and to make representations on what actions we can now take to meet the new loopholes found in the system. On that basis, we received written recordings from the NHO. We received written input from the LO, and as the work on the Document 8 proposal progressed, I would particularly like to thank the two, in addition to the National Association of Building Industries and the Joint Alliance, who have really made heavy contributions to this process. I refer to this in this context, I think, a brilliant example of the professional, political cooperation in Norway and the cooperation between the employees and employers' side in Norway, the document ‘Simple to be serious’, which the National Association of Construction has launched in close cooperation with its employees in the Joint Confederation. On that basis and with the input we have received, we found the basis for promoting 22 concrete actions, and the form in which they have been promoted is an invitation to the government to discuss the 22 actions and use them in the work that needs to be done to meet the challenges. There are some proposals that will just support the central role of the parties in the Norwegian model. I heard that the Scheel Committee – the much-mentioned committee that has looked at corporate taxation and other taxation in Norway – said that there was no good reason for the trade union deduction in the tax. Then Scheel and his selection have not read either Fafo’s large research report on NordMod, the Nordic social model, nor can they have read the report of the Holden III Committee, which then underlines the importance of the role of the parties in the Norwegian society for the Norwegian model in general and for measures against social dumping. That is why we point to strengthening the role of the parties as one of the most powerful steps towards meeting developments. It should be the responsibility of any government to try to contribute to the high level of organisation, both on the employer and employee sides. The same is true of our proposal for the public service scheme, where we say that we should perhaps ease the need for documentation, because it is an instrument that the parties have in place to meet the needs of social dumping. The same is true of the tripartite industry programme, which is a good example, as we have seen in several industries. In construction, we've seen it, and hotels and restaurants and shelters are industries where we're now running programs, which really seem to hit. Then the public sector has an important role to play – an important role because the public sector is a significant employer, but also because the public sector is a significant buyer of services and a significant bidder. Therefore, to help the serious actors, we propose that those who are to have tenders in the public sector should have 25 pst. self-production. They are simply because we don't want to develop – let's say – a mailbox company or the type of bidder that is essentially based on acquiring skills from others, because we know it's vulnerable to social dumping. At the same time, we propose that there should be a maximum of two stages in the contract chain. It turns out that the more terms there are in the contract chain, the more difficult it is to keep an overview, and the more likely it is to be to have underpaid labour. To the extent that there is a deviation from it, a specific justification must be provided. We stress the need for public tenderers to examine the seriousness of the companies submitting tenders. We believe that a serious factor that is very good to measure is the requirement for an apprentice clause, and if the presidency has not found anything new in this period, the Stortinget is an example of a company where an actual apprentice contract is required by those who are to have tenders here on the house. All municipal, county and state companies may be required to do so. One of the measures that I had the pleasure of proposing in my time was joint and several liability, that one is responsible if someone from the subcontractors does not get paid the salary they should have. It was very unpopular that time, but it's very popular among other things. in the Land Association of Construction, yes, so popular that they say it should also apply to builders. We have chosen to propose it to public builders, because we believe that public builders have the resources to ensure that those who are subcontractors of the tender have proper working conditions. These are some examples of the many measures that can be taken by municipalities, counties and countries to use as criteria to develop serious employment in Norway and to make it more difficult for the unprofessional. In that context, I would like to point to the municipality of Skien as a very good example. They have had a full review of their responsibilities and the purchase of services and have established clear criteria for combating social dumping and for strengthening the serious tenderers. So supervision is important. It is true, as the rapporteur initially said, that there is no disagreement about strengthening the interaction between public authorities. We see that as a continuation of the line that we stood for, and as a strengthening of supervision across etat boundaries. Nor are we against stronger sanctions for breaches of the law. We go beyond what the government did in the budget to strengthen it. I would like to stress that, in addition to establishments such as the tax haven, the customs office, the police and the Labour Inspectorate, which are most often mentioned, will for example: Food supervision is an agency that can contribute very much to revealing the unprofessional nature of the restaurant industry, for which we have seen a lot of examples. There is reason to support all such cooperation. In addition, we propose to strengthen the Labour Inspectorate. We also propose, and have put money into it, a separate tip and warning phone, for the most important thing to get to action against social dumping out there is actually that those who discover it have the opportunity to contact a place and get notified about it, so that the authorities can do something about it. In the same way, we are proposing – as a reference to the interpellation that was a little earlier today on the oil and gas sector – a strengthening of Petroleum Supervision. We had earlier, when we were in government, as ambition that we should be world leaders in health, environment and safety. Unfortunately, that ambition has removed the current government, but we believe that it is an area of savings that is now taking place in that sector, where there is particular reason to focus on non-health, non-environment, non-security, but also social dumping being allowed to develop. So cooperation is important. I have mentioned everything about the National Association of Construction and their cooperation with the Joint Confederation, ‘Simple to be serious’, and some of the proposals that lie there, we have included both in the draft document 8 and in our budget. It is developing the ID card scheme, which was unpopular when it was introduced but is currently in demand, and which should actually be improved in order to update the information better and to be more complete in the information. We are proposing several sectoral programmes. We have it in construction, cleaning, where the NHO Service has been a very good co-player. We need it in the hotel and restaurant industries, we need it in the fishing industry – we need it in several areas. Because it turns out that when the parties are engaged through an industry programme, we get results and social dumping is revealed. That is why we find it a pity that the government proposed to move the industry programme away from the department and to the Labour Inspectorate. The Labour Inspectorate shall play an important role in social dumping, but moving the industry programme where we see a lack of interest from the Council of State. Then we are in that situation now that the government and government parties say they see the problem, but so far the government has been passive or gone in the wrong direction. Cooperation with the parties is not, as the case-rapporteur said, a good example of how the government works. Cooperation with the parties is part of what the government has really changed its position on. There is no previous government, nor civil government, which has had such poor cooperation with the parties that the current government has, and I would like to stress all the parties, all the employers' organisations, all the employee organisations and other organisations that former governments, regardless of their colour, have actually used their skills to develop policies, but which are no longer part of them, but are perceived as enemies. We have seen this in the case of the permitting legislation, where the current state council, when he was a magnificant, criticized the then government for providing poor solutions in the permitting legislation, which could lead to increased use of temporaryity, as he said at the time. We're looking at it in the Working Environment Act. We are looking at the weakening of collective law, and we are looking at it as regards equal treatment of collective agreements with regard to the temporary agency directive. That's how the government says it sees the problem, but so far it doesn't look like the government wants to do anything about it.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-15.ana.ud2571e2579	Norway is a small country with small, tidy and transparent conditions. In general, the Norwegian model of society is based on good and functioning democratic principles. This is also reflected in working life. Norwegian working life is basically very good. The vast majority of jobs are characterised by a common will and a common desire to comply with laws and regulations. The Norwegian labour market is characterised by good cooperation and understanding between the parties – joint efforts towards common goals, and a common understanding that involvement in own employment, consensus, cooperation and participation improves both the working environment and productivity. These have been the main lines of Norwegian work and, fortunately, they are still. However, increasing globalisation and internationalisation bring pressure on the Norwegian labour market and also pressure on the working environment. The good cooperation and conditions we have in Norwegian working life are not as prominent in all other countries. Countries with which we have economic cooperation have a different culture of working life than we do. There are also a number of challenges in our main trading area – the EEA. Some of these challenges are best met in cooperation with our international partners, while other challenges are best met at home. As a result, we experience a change in both social and labour-life crime. This may be particularly evident in the light of several foreign actors in the Norwegian labour market. Consequently, it is important that Norwegian authorities closely monitor the situation and are prepared to take the necessary measures to combat social dumping and labour crime. The right-wing Party Government is ready to recognise the importance of combating crime at work and social dumping. The fight against social dumping is also reflected in the political platform between the Right and the Progressive Party. It says, among other things. that we continue to work towards social dumping by strengthening the Labour Inspectorate and evaluating the measures against unacceptable wage and working conditions that have so far been implemented and assessing new measures. Already in the 2015 budget, this is a priority task, with increased appropriations and proposals for joint action by the police, the tax administration and the Labour Inspectorate. But more money and more control are not enough alone. The punishment for the crime of working life must also be sufficiently strict so that it can have a preventative effect. As a result, the Progress Party is very pleased that the Right-Fremskritsparte Government has presented proposals for stricter penalties for breaches of the Labour Environment Act and the Public Service Act. The Right Party, the Advance Party and the Left are also committed to strengthening and further developing cooperation between official control agencies. The Right-wing-Fremskritsparte-Government proposes joint action by the police, the tax administration and the Labour Inspectorate. However, cooperation should not stop with these agencies; it should be possible to strengthen cooperation between several public actors, such as the public sector. UDI, Nav, Fire Service and Food Authority. Fortunately, we have already seen important and good examples of good cooperation between these agencies. The Progress Party is also of the opinion that the introduction of national minimum wages would also contribute positively to the fight against social dumping. Fortunately, this is an exception when labour crime is committed in Norway, and social dumping is also rare in Norwegian workplaces. But it's worrying that it seems to be an increasing trend. The reason for this development may be complex, but increasing internationalisation and migratory pressure, especially in times when economic trends are not so good, may lead to some actors crossing the boundaries of the law. The party welcomes the fact that several representatives are concerned with the issue of social dumping and that they promise this in a separate Document 8. We all have a common goal of combating crime at work and social dumping. Many of the points raised in the proposal will probably come back to the government. But it's important to remember that the government, after all, is doing this important job well. As a result, it becomes a little illogical to ask the government to start a work that has already started, and with which the government is actually about to finish. The Office of the Prime Minister, the Ministry of Labour and Social Affairs, the Ministry of Economic Affairs, the Ministry of Justice, the Ministry of Local Affairs and the Ministry of Finance are already working well with a comprehensive strategy against social dumping and against labour crime. The Prime Minister has also had several meetings, including: LO and NHO on the issue, and the government will soon present a comprehensive action plan against labour crime and social dumping.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-15.ana.ud2571e3134	There is an increasing claim that Norway needs more foreign labour in the future. ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-15.ana.ud2571e14150	There is a vote on proposal No. 1, from the Workers' Party and the Socialist Left Party. The proposal reads as follows: ‘The government, in cooperation with the social partners, is asked to prepare and submit to the Stortinget an action plan against social dumping and crime at work in Norway, which should include the following measures and instruments: Strengthen the role of the social partners and stimulate an increase in the level of organisation of employers and workers, for example by increasing the trade-union deduction. Improvement of the public utility scheme, for example by simplifying the documentation requirement. Establish a strong plan to strengthen and coordinate the efforts of official control agencies and the police against social dumping and crime at work. Strengthening the Labour Inspectorate, for example with new instruments, establishing ‘first-line supervision’, several unannounced supervision, reinforced sanctions and increased financial frameworks. Create and operate a tip and alert phone — with opening hours 0600–2400 — on social dumping and work-life crime under the direction of the Labour Inspectorate. Strengthening Petroleum Supervision, for example with new instruments, several unannounced supervision, reinforced sanctions and increased financial frameworks. Increase the penalties and fines for social dumping and labour crime and improve the criminal justice chain in these kinds of cases. Suggest changes to the Foreign Affairs Act so that anyone who exploits a foreigner’s situation improperly can be punished. Strengthen legislation and practice to avoid ‘bankruptcy riders’. Information on ‘bankruptcy riders’ needs to be made more widely available between the registers of public agencies. To require a maximum of two stages in the contract chain in public tenders. Reasons shall be given for any deviation. Demand for a minimum of 25 per cent self-production by the main contractor. Public purchasers must require the seriousness and orderly pay and working conditions of all their suppliers, such as improved documentary checks, tax and tax certificates, checks against the employee register, the VAT register, etc. Introduce apprenticeship clauses in public procurement, i.e. that establishments supplying to the public sector must demonstrate that they have an apprenticeship scheme and that they have apprentices employed on projects where possible. Develop better document and ID control systems, for example when issuing D-numbers, and consider using ID cards in several industries. Develop more and more targeted sectoral programmes/schemes, for example in the fishing industry. Establish a new central approval and register system for the construction industry and establish a qualification register and associated measures – in line with the proposals in the report ‘Simple to be serious’ from BNL [[The vote will be taken at 5 p.m. 21.59.07]]
